I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I wouldn’t say depressed exactly, maybe just worn down? What would count as “trouble sleeping,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything, because I do try to keep up.\n\nBut when I’m supposed to just focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, my mind kind of… branches off. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your worrying still feel mostly driven by the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the lab changes, yeah… or that’s the easiest thing to point to.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, it doesn’t really stay there. Yeah… I think most days, yes.

I’ll tell myself, like, “Just focus on the report,” or “just check the assay and move on,” and that works for maybe a second.\n\nBut then one worry kind of turns into another, and pretty soon I’m just staring at my screen, not really doing anything, while my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong. It’s… hard to shut off, I guess. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long has this level of worrying been going on?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about seven months now. It kind of started after the lab’s funding shift and the commercial partnership happened, when the way everything was being run changed. I told myself it was just an adjustment period, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… yeah, around seven months.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the changes at work, or have they spread well beyond that?\n\nPatient: It started with the work changes, yeah… but I don’t think it’s only that anymore. It’s kind of spread out.\n\nLike, I’ll start worrying about whether the new partnership means my position could get cut, but then it jumps to whether my data are wrong, or whether bills are going to pile up, or if my family thinks I’m falling behind. And then I end up thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nI know some of that might sound a little over the top… but that’s sort of how it’s been going.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been making it harder for you to do your work or handle things day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still getting things done mostly, or I’m trying to. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just dropping everything.\n\nBut… it has started to get in the way. I missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and the data review organized. I kept going back over things and then not actually finishing it.\n\nAnd outside of work, even normal stuff feels harder than it should. Like preparing reports, showing up to art nights, cooking for my family… things I usually could keep up with now feel like too much sometimes. I know that probably sounds kind of basic, but it’s been hard to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things, like your data or reports, more than you feel you need to?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it “repeatedly” exactly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “trouble” versus just normal bad sleep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Over the past seven months, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most days, yes.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m panicking every second, but it’s been pretty constant. That’s actually kind of why I came in — my mind just keeps jumping around. Like, I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then it turns into whether my data are wrong, or if my bills are going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind… and then I’m thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo, yeah. Most days sounds accurate, unfortunately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this anxiety would still be this strong if the changes at the lab had never happened?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. The lab changes definitely gave my brain something concrete to latch onto, so I don’t want to pretend they didn’t matter.\n\nBut it’s not just that, if I’m being honest. It sort of jumps from that to everything else — like whether my position could get cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills, then whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, then grad school and whether I’ve already messed that up. So even if the partnership hadn’t happened, I think my mind would’ve found other things to keep circling around, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep intruding into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call them “intrusive” in a dramatic way, but they do kind of push in even when I’m trying to concentrate.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just finish this report, or just check this assay, and then one worry leads into another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. I try to pull myself back, but it doesn’t always work. Does that count as what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And then I end up thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nI know some of that might sound a little over the top… but that’s sort of how it’s been going.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been making it harder for you to do your work or handle things day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still getting things done mostly, or I’m trying to. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just dropping everything.\n\nBut… it has started to get in the way. I missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and the data review organized. I kept going back over things and then not actually finishing it.\n\nAnd outside of work, even normal stuff feels harder than it should. Like preparing reports, showing up to art nights, cooking for my family… things I usually could keep up with now feel like too much sometimes. I know that probably sounds kind of basic, but it’s been hard to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things, like your data or reports, more than you feel you need to?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it “repeatedly” exactly. I mean, I do look back over things because I don’t want to turn in something sloppy, but I’m not sure if it’s more than I need to.\n\nMaybe sometimes I’ll reread a report or check the numbers again, but that also feels kind of normal for lab work, right? I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I wouldn’t say depressed exactly, maybe just worn down? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve had some low moments, I guess, but I’m not sure if that counts as feeling depressed. Maybe I’m just stressed. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’m… not sure I’d say that. I’ve had days where I feel pretty low or kind of discouraged, but “sad most days” sounds stronger than I’d put it.\n\nI guess it’s more like I feel worn out and tense, and then sometimes that makes me feel down. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but at work I do feel kind of wound up a lot.\n\nLike I can’t really settle into my chair, so I’ll be tapping my foot or checking messages, or I’ll walk back to the instrument room even when I don’t really have a reason to. And sometimes normal lab noises make me jump more than they probably should.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean by on edge, but… yeah, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sleep? I’m not totally sure. I mean, I wouldn’t want to say it’s a major problem if it isn’t.\n\nI guess I don’t really have a clear answer. Some nights are probably better than others, but I haven’t been tracking it or anything. What would count as “trouble sleeping,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything, because I do try to keep up.\n\nBut when I’m supposed to just focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, my mind kind of… branches off. One worry leads to another, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the screen while I’m thinking through every possible way something could go wrong.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess concentration has been harder than it should be. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your worrying still feel mostly driven by the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the lab changes, yeah… or that’s the easiest thing to point to.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, it doesn’t really stay there. My mind kind of jumps from, like, whether this new partnership means my position could get cut, to whether my data are wrong, and then somehow I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or if I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot. I’m not trying to be dramatic, it just… doesn’t stay in one lane.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lot of muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but my shoulders and neck are pretty much tight most of the day.\n\nI’ll be working on spreadsheets or something and then notice I’m clenching my jaw without realizing it. By the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly been at my desk. Is that the kind of muscle tension you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I sleep, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nFalling asleep can take a while sometimes, and I do wake up here and there, but I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “trouble” versus just normal bad sleep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Over the past seven months, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most days, yes.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m panicking every second, but it’s been pretty constant. That’s actually kind of why I came in — my mind just keeps jumping around. Like, I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then it turns into whether my data are wrong, or if my bills are going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind… and then I’m thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo, yeah. Most days sounds accurate, unfortunately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this anxiety would still be this strong if the changes at the lab had never happened?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. It sort of jumps from that to everything else — like whether my position could get cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills, then whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, then grad school and whether I’ve already messed that up. So even if the partnership hadn’t happened, I think my mind would’ve found other things to keep circling around, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep intruding into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call them “intrusive” in a dramatic way, but they do kind of push in even when I’m trying to concentrate.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just finish this report, or just check this assay, and then one worry leads into another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. I try to pull myself back, but it doesn’t always work. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel senseless or out of proportion to you, even while they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know some of it is probably out of proportion. I can see that, especially when it jumps so fast from one thing to the next.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind just keeps going from, “What if the lab’s new partnership means my position gets cut?” to “What if my data are wrong?” and then bills, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. When I say it out loud, it does sound like… a lot.\n\nBut in the moment it doesn’t feel silly, exactly. I missed an internal lab deadline because I couldn’t get my writing and the data review organized. I kept going back over things and then not actually finishing it.\n\nAnd outside of work, even normal stuff feels harder than it should. Like preparing reports, showing up to art nights, cooking for my family… things I usually could keep up with now feel like too much sometimes. I know that probably sounds kind of basic, but it’s been hard to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things, like your data or reports, more than you feel you need to?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it “repeatedly” exactly. I mean, I do look back over things because I don’t want to turn in something sloppy, but I’m not sure if it’s more than I need to.\n\nMaybe sometimes I’ll reread a report or check the numbers again, but that also feels kind of normal for lab work, right? I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I wouldn’t say depressed exactly, maybe just worn down? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve had some low moments, I guess, but I’m not sure if that counts as feeling depressed. Maybe I’m just stressed. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’m… not sure I’d say that. I’ve had days where I feel pretty low or kind of discouraged, but “sad most days” sounds stronger than I’d put it.\n\nI guess it’s more like I feel worn out and tense, and then sometimes that makes me feel down. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but at work I do feel kind of wound up a lot.\n\nLike I can’t really settle into my chair, so I’ll be tapping my foot or checking messages, or I’ll walk back to the instrument room even when I don’t really have a reason to. And sometimes normal lab noises make me jump more than they probably should.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean by on edge, but… yeah, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sleep? I’m not totally sure. I mean, I wouldn’t want to say it’s a major problem if it isn’t.\n\nI guess I don’t really have a clear answer. Some nights are probably better than others, but I haven’t been tracking it or anything. What would count as “trouble sleeping,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job or anything, because I do try to keep up.\n\nBut when I’m supposed to just focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, my mind kind of… branches off. One worry leads to another, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the screen while I’m thinking through every possible way something could go wrong.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess concentration has been harder than it should be. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your worrying still feel mostly driven by the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: I think it started with the lab changes, yeah… or that’s the easiest thing to point to.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, it doesn’t really stay there. My mind kind of jumps from, like, whether this new partnership means my position could get cut, to whether my data are wrong, and then somehow I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or if I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot. I’m not trying to be dramatic, it just… doesn’t stay in one lane.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lot of muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but my shoulders and neck are pretty much tight most of the day.\n\nI’ll be working on spreadsheets or something and then notice I’m clenching my jaw without realizing it. By the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly been at my desk. Is that the kind of muscle tension you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I sleep, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nFalling asleep can take a while sometimes, and I do wake up here and there, but I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “trouble” versus just normal bad sleep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Over the past seven months, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most days, yes.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m panicking every second, but it’s been pretty constant. That’s actually kind of why I came in — my mind just keeps jumping around. Like, I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then it turns into whether my data are wrong, or if my bills are going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind… and then I’m thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo, yeah. Most days sounds accurate, unfortunately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this anxiety would still be this strong if the changes at the lab had never happened?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. The lab changes definitely gave my brain something concrete to latch onto, so I don’t want to pretend they didn’t matter.\n\nBut it’s not just that, if I’m being honest. It sort of jumps from that to everything else — like whether my position could get cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills, then whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, then grad school and whether I’ve already messed that up. So even if the partnership hadn’t happened, I think my mind would’ve found other things to keep circling around, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep intruding into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call them “intrusive” in a dramatic way, but they do kind of push in even when I’m trying to concentrate.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, okay, just finish this report, or just check this assay, and then one worry leads into another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. I try to pull myself back, but it doesn’t always work. Does that count as what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel senseless or out of proportion to you, even while they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know some of it is probably out of proportion. I can see that, especially when it jumps so fast from one thing to the next.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind just keeps going from, “What if the lab’s new partnership means my position gets cut?” to “What if my data are wrong?” and then bills, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. When I say it out loud, it does sound like… a lot.\n\nBut in the moment it doesn’t feel silly, exactly. It feels like I need to solve it right then, even if part of me knows I’m probably overdoing it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel like you have to do something over and over—like checking, counting, repeating, or mentally reviewing—to make the anxiety go down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like counting or repeating things.\n\nI do sometimes go back over things in my head, but it feels more like trying to reassure myself or make sure I’m not missing something, not like a ritual I have to do a certain number of times. I’m not sure if that’s different or if I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would, honestly.\n\nI’d like to say it’s just the lab situation, because that would be easier to explain. But even when I try to focus on that one thing, my mind kind of jumps around—from whether this partnership could mean my position gets cut, to whether my data are wrong, then bills, then whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo… I don’t think fixing the lab thing would make all of it stop. It might help, but I think my brain would just find the next thing to worry about. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
